ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U4	Thank you, good manager. Prime Minister! When we saw the inflation figure in December that annual inflation was 12.2%, we started to think that we hadn't seen such a number for many decades. January was no more pleasant than inflation of 11.3%. Food prices increased by 11%, transport prices by 17% and housing costs by 31.5%. All these product groups and service groups are very difficult to replace. And many of these costs are those that cannot be avoided. However, the costs that can be avoided, such as entertainment, cultural costs, are in sectors that have been hit very badly in the crisis anyway. And we know from the past that people are also gonna stick together in dental care. It is an expensive service, but it is actually very necessary, with long-term health effects, but something that can be abandoned if food prices make such an increase as they do now. We're talking about an increase in the average wage. And indeed, in recent years, the increase in average wages has exceeded the price increase. At the same time, we need to see very clearly [see] regional inequalities. In Võrumaa, Valgamaa, Ida-Virumaa, Jõgeva County, half the people on the islands earn less than 1000 euros and have little savings. So the situation is that in the store we all pay a higher price for food and pay more for heating and electricity with our bills. Housing costs have also increased for those who rent home. There are significantly fewer recipients of wage increases than those who already feel the current cost pressure. Does the government see the problem and what is being done to ensure that people survive in such an inflationary environment?
ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U5	Thank you. Indeed, price growth has been unexpectedly rapid in recent months and this inflation has had a direct impact on people. One side is the appreciation of energy carriers that we have wrestled to help people. What is positive is that Eesti Pank estimates that these support measures for energy [costs] have helped to contain inflation, but not enough, of course. It is also positive that wages have increased faster than inflation, but this is probably not directly helping people today. If you look at the experts' assessments, it is considered that this [tendence] is not long-term. At some point it turns and energy prices, which affect all other inputs, should still be either a downward trend, but at least stay. This could slow the overall price increase. It is also positive that when we take economic recovery, when the economy is doing well, people are doing well too. And the economy has recovered well. Eesti Pank has said that this price cap for households, when we talk about energy, has helped to curb the rise in prices. The economic situation in Estonia is quite good, the economic recovery has been faster than in many other countries, but therefore inflation has also been faster. If you look at the long term, it is also positive that in the long term, if Estonian prices have increased by an average of 28% over the last ten years, the wage has risen by 92% and the minimum wage has even increased by 125%. This is in retrospect, but specifically in terms of inflation, we are following very closely. And, of course, we are trying to help precisely those who are most affected, who, as you say, are unable to cut off spending on food and household expenditure. So the tools that we have: one way is to increase income support to help those people who are the hardest, for whom the price increase is very important. The second option is to look at what we can still do here, given that the price increase is not only dependent on Estonia, but also, unfortunately, on many factors that are out of our control.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U7	Thank you. Indeed, price increases are not only dependent on Estonia and not all factors are under our control. ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U8	Thank you. Indeed, the people you referred to are the hardest. As far as pensioners are concerned, we have taken quite a number of steps to help pensioners. Firstly, the benefit of a single-living pensioner will rise from October, and will rise significantly. Secondly, the average pension was exempt from income tax, which means that this part of pensions will not be deducted either, which means that more money will be left to the person to cope with his or her expenses. And thirdly, extraordinary pension increases have been planned, and pensions will rise from next year [more than indexation is foreseen]. Now, as far as families are concerned, I very much agree that it will be harder for those who also spend on underage children, especially single parents, to cope. Once again, it should help them specifically to have targeted measures that help these people in particular. Every time we do something with a certain amount of money, there's nothing else we can do with that money. Therefore, keeping the focus on those people who need help the most is, in my opinion, very important. What are the tools to avoid so many people in relative poverty? Income support is one of them, [higher] pensions for the elderly are another such option and indeed, these three steps we have already taken. When it comes to subsidies for energy prices, the measure is also specifically targeted at those with lower incomes. For those who have applied for [the aid] at the moment when I last asked, the average amount applied for was EUR 130. That's a lot of money. After seven months, there is an opportunity to apply for this benefit, which will reduce the price increase in order to save its costs. The state is helping to cope with these costs. But yes, you have to look further at the families with children, but not all families with children need help. Help must be directed at those who really need it.

In particular, on 15 August 2019, when Isamaa was in government, an amendment to the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act was initiated, which excluded national comprehensive school finals from the conditions of graduation. This amendment was intended to give the school as a whole the competence to decide whether to complete comprehensive school. It was coordinated by all Ministers of the Fatherland: Minister for Defence, Minister for Culture, Minister for Foreign Affairs and Minister for Justice. Just so you're clear, you've come up with a bill like this. But I'm coming to the other side now. The comprehensive school finals are compulsory and will remain compulsory. You can't finish comprehensive school without taking your exams. It is very important that a graduate of primary school can speak the official language. At the moment, grade 3 is the threshold for the comprehensive school final, but the language skills of graduates are less than we would like. This amendment has been presented to the partners for discussion. This means that we cannot talk about it in any way. It has been presented to discuss how to deal with the situation. The regulation on the coordination round proposes changes in the way comprehensive school finals are assessed, both for Estonian and Russian students. It is proposed that the result be entered in the final certificate as a percentage of the maximum result rather than setting the threshold. It is important that completion of comprehensive school continues to require that the final annual scores for all subjects are at least satisfactory, with [knowledge acquired at least] at "3'. So you can't finish comprehensive school without [knowledge]. When we talk about Russian-speaking schools, I will probably still be able to answer your question further, then, in fact, at the moment, this level has been very low. We should discuss among ourselves how to raise the level of proficiency in Estonian for middle school graduates. This is done not by one exam, but by consistent teaching and by the fact that we have teachers, we have methodologies, and the Estonian language is taught in a consistent manner.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U43	Thank you for your question. Indeed I can. The way this topic has been mediated through the media indeed, there may have been too much noise in it. As regards the granting of additional rights to law enforcement officials in local governments, this issue has been topical in quite a number of recent governments. Home affairs ministers, different interior ministers, have dealt with this. The question is how the local authority can perform the existing tasks even better. When we talk about the additional rights and competences of [government] law enforcement officers, this is about creating an opportunity, not making an obligation. I think that is the most important point in this matter. It has never been worth putting the local government on any additional task, but giving it an opportunity. If the local government has a desire to maintain a sobering house itself, then [it is possible]. For example, Tallinn's sobering house is built by the local government, it is maintained, the police provide this service. The police provide this service because the municipal law enforcement officers simply do not have such rights, and that is why the police have been implemented to carry out their duties. This package has been much wider than that, not only is it possible for the local government, if it has such a desire, to take the person caught by the gears of life to the sobering house, if he is intoxicated and unable to take care of himself, is dangerous to himself and can be dangerous to others. Some municipalities have this opportunity today, but there is no right. There is a municipal police in Tallinn, for example, who would have an interest and a desire to take care of a person who needs such help and bring it to the sobering house, but the law does not just give this right today. And, of course, when we talk about transporting a person to a sobering house, a law enforcement officer, a municipal law enforcement officer, must be guaranteed the possibility of defending himself if necessary. We are also talking about the possibilities for certain coercive measures to be implemented by a municipal law enforcement officer. Now, if this is the will of some municipalities, the legal space does not allow it. Although I have introduced the possibilities of moving forward with this in my office, there is no possibility of doing so today. But if this option existed in our law and the local government had a wish, then these law enforcement officers should, of course, undergo further training. It's what the National Defence Academy offers. If I remember correctly, there must be a category six qualification of a law enforcement officer, proper uniforms, equipment and training. This also requires certain investments. This training at the National Defence Academy costs 1500 euros for one person. If the local government wants to create such a service or capacity, then the form equipment, other equipment, training to access it will be somewhere 7 000 euros per person. But once again, this is an opportunity offer, not an obligation. That's all that matters. I have met a lot with various mayors, a great deal of support for [this idea], an urban-municipal association in itself supports the granting of additional rights to local government law enforcement officials, if they wish to do so themselves. It's just a matter of law to create this opportunity. Once again, an opportunity must be created. One more example. In Tallinn, a police patrol spends 7,000 10,000 hours a year sobering people only to take the person between the gears to the sobering house. In fact, the police could use these hours of work, for example, for traffic surveillance or follow-up to close-up violence. And if the local government has expressed its wish, then it could be involved in the transportation of people who are drinking alcohol. If that's what they want.

So it cannot be said that we have treated our [pig breeders] very badly. When we think about the preferences of Estonian consumers, 50% of the [muscle] that is consumed per person is pork. ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U71	Thank you, Honourable Asker. I'm trying to explain. I will explain to you: because this [branch of production] or activity is still clearly regarded as an industrial activity. This means that it can be restored quickly, on the one hand, and there are business models that are limited, for example, to buying in feed and getting out of production. But Estonian pig farmers have one particular feature. If we compare them with European [pig farmers], we will see that Estonian pig farmers have little land. And that actually means that they do not receive any of the other subsidies that their competitors receive. This also means that they have more difficulties in managing cash flows and are more dependent on the effects of the market, especially on multiple effects. In the Council of Europe, 19 countries also raised questions to the Commission that in the light of what has happened in Europe, including Estonia, additional financial assistance could actually be provided at European level. However, this has not been accepted, and the Commission actually thinks of it by referring in part to these aspects. Another important thing is that if we look at a possible energy revolution in the future related to the common agricultural policy, it is extremely important that more alternatives to imported feed are found both in Estonia and elsewhere in Europe. This means whether we can find additional ways to develop protein crops in order to lower the prices of inputs and be less dependent in this respect. But, indeed, animal welfare support and breeding aid are part of the aid that we can actually offer to the a priori sector on an ongoing basis. The government, of course, also proceeds from this so-called crisis situation and, if there are certain anomalies, deep crises, the [their effects] will be mitigated. But if we take the money that has been paid out in the short term now, it will be close to 10% of the sales value of the same sector.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U136	Last year, the Government of the Republic approved four development plans for the Ministry of Education and Research. They are based directly on the Estonian state's long-term strategy "Estonia 2035," which will guide our country's decisions for the next 13 years, as a basis for planning euro grants and drawing up the state budget. I am honoured to be the first minister to the Riigikogu to give you an overview of the strategic developments in the field. The goal of "Estonia 2035" is a smart, active and healthy Estonian person. The development plan for the field of education 2021 to 2035 sets the objective that Estonian people have knowledge, skills and attitudes that enable them to exercise themselves in personal life, work and society. In order to achieve this objective, we must ensure that learning opportunities are selective and accessible and that the education system allows for smooth movement between levels of education and members. We must also ensure that there are competent and motivated teachers and school leaders in Estonia, a diverse learning environment and learning from the learner. We must bear in mind that learning opportunities meet the development needs of society and the labour market. For the last two years, the whole world has had to cope with a global corona virus pandemic that has had both negative and positive consequences for our education system. Last spring, Harju and Ida-Virumaa were 10/11. Class 1 students 15 weeks, or 75 days of study distance study, and grade 5 and 8 children 13 weeks, or 65 days of study. All over Estonia were the smallest, that is, 1/4. Class 1 children [Distance studies] totaled seven weeks, i.e. 35 study days and 9 graders for nine weeks. Distance learning has created learning gaps and constantly changing circumstances and uncertainty have also affected the mental health of young people. Analysis by the United Nations, the World Bank, the European Commission and UNESCO will assess that there have been significant learning gaps due to school closure and distance learning, as distance learning is less effective than contact learning. This is also evidenced, for example, by the results of our comprehensive school finals. For all three exams, the average score was lower in 2021 than in 2016/2019. the annual average. Researchers and experts estimate that learning gaps have a cumulative character. The decline is increasing in the following years of study. Learning motivation decreases, leading to early school leaving, which in turn leads to social costs and reduces people's success in the labour market. Based on previous studies, the OECD has assessed that the expected life-cycle average income of students affected by school closure and distance learning is 3% lower than would have been without a pandemic. For countries, this could mean an average of 1.5% lower annual GDP over the rest of the century. During this academic year, we have made a lot of efforts to avoid all-Estonian outright distance learning. Thanks to the rapid testing in schools since November, we were able to keep schools largely open during the last delta strain wave. On the basis of feedback from schools, students and parents, we now know that this has been the right approach. In order to level out the educational gaps, school holders have received a total of EUR 17 million to provide students with additional support, depending on their needs, whether individual consultations, training in smaller groups, extra hours or courses. With the extra money, there has been an opportunity to hire assistant teachers. Schools can invite students to help with the help of assistant teachers and teachers through a substitute teacher program. We have allocated a total of half a million euros to this last year and this year. In total, schools have already needed support for these programmes for more than 12,500 hours. This year we added further education to the public training order, i.e. training courses offered free of charge by the state in order to prepare teaching aids that could prevent and level out the educational gaps of pupils in general education schools. For graduates, we ordered training courses in test subjects from universities. Last spring almost 7,500 graduates took part and these courses received very good feedback. By this spring we have also ordered online courses in preparation for final exams from Tallinn University, Tallinn University of Technology and the University of Tartu for half a million euros. Compared to last year, we significantly expanded the range of courses, for example in chemistry and physics. In total, we have ordered 11 preparatory online courses from universities, which are about to begin. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! We know from studies and feedback from teachers that long-term distance learning last spring had a negative impact on student motivation. In spring, we supported 504 learning-interest camps with EUR 7.3 million, with more than 63000 children from all over Estonia. Mental health issues in general need to be addressed intensively in the context of a pandemic. In last year's school satisfaction polls, almost half of the elevenths said that they were often or always sick of everything. According to the same study, 26% of teachers often or always experienced stress. Only a third of the teachers said that you rarely or never experienced stress. The number of juvenile suicides that doubled during the Coronary Apandemia is particularly worrying. What have we been able to do? In the spring of last year, in cooperation with the School Psychologists' Association, we opened a free helpline of 1226 to help students, educators and parents. Each week, online seminars will take place "From Distance to Smarter" where teachers and school leaders will receive practical recommendations both to make teaching more effective and to support students' mental health. In addition, we have provided first-aid mental health training for youth workers. We ordered a series of mental health lectures from the Association of Estonian Folk Universities and the Minutes of Silence for parents to increase parents' awareness and competence in supporting children's mental health and learning during the crisis and post-crisis period. In total, more than 100 training courses have taken place and web seminars have been completed. The training was highly valued, which is why we are planning to continue. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Over the past two years, stakeholders in education have been required to adapt quickly, to acquire new skills, to cope flexibly with rapidly changing circumstances. This crisis has also been a kind of acceleration of development in order to test flexible learning and assessment methods, to focus on the student and to transform the study work into a student-driven approach, as foreseen by the educational development plan. However, our most important goal in the near future is to come out of the corona crisis as safely as possible and to take all the important lessons from this crisis. The focus continues to be on the quality of education and the well-being of the learner, because a child who is stressed or frightened cannot learn according to his or her abilities. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The intelligent person named as the goal of the "Estonia 2035" development strategy is curious, creative and entrepreneurial, wants to learn and is ready to change the nature of the work. The education sector needs teachers who are willing to work towards this goal. According to OSKA, Estonia needs to replace around 380 teachers of general education schools per year because of age. The situation with science subjects and mathematics teachers is particularly critical in schools. One in five mathematics, chemistry, geography and biology teachers are at least 60 years old and one in four physics teachers are at that age. We cannot offer modern learning in schools if we do not have enough qualified and motivated teachers at school. In 2020, we discussed the topic of teacher follow-up in this very Chamber of the Riigikogu as an important national issue. We have brought together the proposals of the Committee on Culture and the results of a broad-based working group in the follow-up action plan for teachers, which will frame all activities in this area until 2026. The action plan was based on two major problems: not enough new qualified teachers were added and too many teachers were quitting or were planning to leave. The action plan envisages improving the quality of school management, more flexible qualifications for teachers, reviewing the workload of school staff, supporting educators throughout their careers and improving the image of the teaching profession. Our goal is that in 2026 all schools have managers with top management competences, and all schools are learning organisations. Changing the recruitment system for school leaders is also an important issue. To this end, we have started discussions with representatives of the school leaders' association and universities, including other related parties. We are certainly also talking here about the need to increase the competitiveness of the salary of the head of school as a top manager. Support for a starting teacher is also an important issue. Estonian education statistics show that in the last ten years, 74% of teachers who started in general education schools worked at school during the following academic year, and in the fifth year, only one other teacher who started in school continued in office. We want to increase the differentiation component of teachers' salaries from 17.1% to at least 20% of the education allowance for schoolchildren. This target will require an additional EUR 12 million to the state budget. This would make it possible, for example, to compensate for the lower hours spent by the starting teacher in order to get into school life during the first years of his work, and also to reward the mentor who supports the starting teacher. It would also provide the school manager with more flexible opportunities to support the teachers' career model. We are currently in a situation where more and more of the starting teachers do not meet the qualifications requirements. They need to be given flexible opportunities to find teachers' training in addition to working, or to develop competence to apply for a teaching profession. The aim is to create a high-quality model of initial and continuing training for teachers and support professionals, which takes better account of the need to learn alongside work and family life. Together with the University of Tallinn and the University of Tartu, we are setting up a programme of the Academy of Teachers, which aims to support the subsequent growth of teachers and support specialists by providing flexible initial and refresher training, to ensure the secondary growth of teaching staff and to develop high-level educational research. We would like all unskilled teachers to be supported in choosing a study path and acquiring an occupation. There is also a need for continuous support and further training for all teachers. We have signed agreements with both the University of Tartu and the University of Tallinn to help universities create 141 additional teaching places in teacher training disciplines in the following academic year, with additional funding. In total, we want to create at least 550 new teachers' training places in 2022 to 2025. The pay of the Estonian teacher compared to purchasing power is the second in the OECD's European countries. The minimum wage for teachers rose from 1 January 2002 to 1412 euros, or 7.3%. The total wage increase was 30 million euros. In the first year, the average salary of Estonian teachers is 1653 euros, which is now comparable to the average monthly wage in Estonia. But the goal is to reach an average wage of 2336 euros by 2025, which is estimated to be 120% of the average gross wage in Estonia. This will require EUR 63.2 million to be added to the state budget next year, and EUR 125.2 million in 2024. A total of EUR 365,1 million will be needed for this in three years. The follow-up action plan for teachers is a concrete and clear work programme, the activities of which we have already started and we will continue to have children and young people in schools and kindergartens to teach and support sufficient numbers of qualified and motivated teachers in the future. School leaders should be seen as top managers in the public sector and teachers as top specialists. Their remuneration must correspond to that. The future of our country depends very much on how we value educators. The Committee on Culture has consensually assessed the importance of investing teachers' salaries. I continue to count on the support of the political parties of the Riigikogu on this subject. Honourable listeners! On the International Youth Day, the government approved the Youth Development Plan 2021-2035, which sets the goal that the development opportunities, security and solid support offered to young people will create Estonia, which young people want to move forward. To this end, young people, their solutions and ideas need to be taken into account, to discover and develop the talents and strengths of young people, and to support the entrepreneurship and creativity of young people. It is also necessary to create prerequisites for young people's confidence in the state, to listen to young people at all levels of government, and to empower young people's active citizenship. We have developed a concept for the development of talent, and the final line is to draw up an action plan for the observation of talent and the development of talent, which will provide the basis for a comprehensive system to support talented learners of all ages. The current view of the awareness and development of talented students has remained too narrowly central to Olympiads and in-depth courses, often ignoring less competitive and younger children. Here you must remember: every child is talented. Anne needs to be discovered and developed. In autumn last year, a two-year project began, creating a framework for closer integration of youth work, education of interest and activities of interest with general and vocational education. Our aim is to make the education system more flexible and student-centred so that the skills and knowledge acquired in recreational education can also be taken into account in formal education. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The "Estonia 2035" development strategy says that all social groups must have equal opportunities. The aim of the Estonian Language Development Plan 2021 to 2035 is to ensure the vitality and functioning of the Estonian language as the first language in every area of life in Estonia. In addition, the development plan says that by 2035 mostly education in Estonian has passed, i.e.: teaching in schools and kindergartens funded by the state and local authorities takes place in Estonian. Education in Estonian guarantees everyone an equal opportunity to participate in social and professional life and to continue their studies at the next level of education. Good listeners! All young people studying in our primary schools must be able to continue at the next level of education without hindrance. During this academic year, a little more than 21000 students, or almost 16% of all primary school graduates, complete basic education in Russian. In Estonia there are 20 comprehensive schools with a purely Russian language of instruction and 37 language immersion schools. As the second language of Estonian, the results of the comprehensive school final exams were lower last spring than in the previous three years. In 2021 the average score was 64.7%, before the average score was 70%. The results of language immersion classes and language exams are nearly 20% higher than those of Russian students in schools, remaining 90% of the border countries. So the immersion works. Last year, an action plan for education in Estonian was completed, which is marked because in the past, the necessary steps in this area have not been described in such detail. The action plan was born in cooperation with the representatives of kindergartens, schools and universities and umbrella organisations, who are in contact with Estonian and other native language children and young people in their daily work and teach in Estonian. Educational tools and methodologies should be developed and teachers trained, including the development of the skills of existing teachers for teaching pupils of different ages, with different cultural and educational backgrounds. The additional requirement for teachers of Estonian as a second language is 26 teachers per year orientingly during the 2022-2028 period. If we have teachers at school today who cannot speak Estonian, we cannot expect them to be able to provide education in Estonian. At the end of last year, the Language Board mapped the training needs of existing Estonian language teachers. The needs of pre-school children's institutions, general education as well as of the education staff of hobbies were assessed. And the result? In total, it is estimated that 2800.3100 educators are required for language training. Their language level is not sufficient. The main focus of the action plan is on training competent teachers. In order to alleviate the shortage of teachers in a multilingual school, we already allocated money to the University of Tartu Narva College this year to create 28 additional places for admission to the next academic year. There are currently around 10,000 children in kindergartens with a Russian language, or 14% of children in kindergartens. In total, there are 97 Russian language kindergartens in Estonia during this academic year. Today we have 152 additional Estonian-language teachers in the Russian kindergartens in 60 kindergartens. Starting in the year, 3.67 million euros have been allocated to early childhood education and in schools where there are already 40 additional teachers in Estonian today, 1.43 million euros. I'm sure we want to expand the "+1 teacher" program. We have also expanded the language immersion programme. According to the Estonian Education Information System, there were more than 3700 children in the language immersion of kindergarten, and more than 8000 children in primary school. We support the language immersion programme, including the education of children of new immigrants in schools with EUR 2 million. The number of students with different mother tongues from Estonian has increased significantly in schools with Estonian language of instruction in the last five years. This year, the state gave almost half a million euros to 23 comprehensive schools with Estonian language of instruction to support further education in Estonian for middle school students with other mother tongues. All comprehensive schools with at least 10% of students in other languages received support. For every student in need of additional support in learning Estonian, we have allocated 330 euros so that schools can offer a faster adaptation program to cope with Estonian-language learning. With the support, support for the Estonian language can be offered to nearly 1500 students and we also want to expand this programme. Narva Estonian Gymnasium was managed by the Ministry of Education and Research. The transfer of the school to the state is an important step towards the establishment of the Estonian-language educational campus in Narva, where in a few years' time there will be an opportunity to study in Estonian from kindergarten to the end of the upper secondary school. The state invests 30 million euros in the circa educational campus with a modern learning environment. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! We launched an action plan for education in Estonian to give everyone an equal opportunity to participate in social and professional life and to continue their studies at the next level of education. We have already developed and are planning to continue to allocate the necessary funding to kindergartens and schools, which will enable training in Estonian to be organised, ensuring high-quality education. We are working to ensure that additional teachers with appropriate qualifications reach educational institutions. Good listeners! The "Estonia 2035" development strategy also aims to create favourable conditions for business R &amp; D and innovation. Researchers and businesses cooperate with each other. Our Estonian development plan for research and development, innovation and entrepreneurship aims to increase the well-being and economic productivity of Estonian society. We are essentially faced with an extremely large and responsible task, because we need to ensure long-term and consistent productivity growth through investment in a system of scientific development. This means not only applying research results to the benefit of the economy, but also increasing the effectiveness of science and shaping strategic research trends that meet society's development needs. Funding for science and higher education is critical. Research funding must remain at 1% of gross domestic product. Compared to last year, more than €40 million was added to support R &amp; D and innovation this year. The total amount of R &amp; D expenditure in the state budget in 2022 is €323.7 million. It is important to design the R &amp; D system so that private R &amp; D investment also grows rapidly. This will allow for an increase in the number of researchers and engineers in the business sector at least three times. The procedure of the Riigikogu is currently the reform of doctoral studies, which provides the majority of doctoral students with a junior researcher's post or [opportunity] to conclude a contract of employment in a non-university institution in the field related to their doctoral thesis. The transition to the new concept of doctoral studies will take place gradually until 2026. I hope that this bill will also receive support from the Riigikogu. Unlike research funding, funding for higher education has remained in the way of time over the years. Despite the fact that higher education expenditure has increased by 22.4% compared to 2012, to compensate for paid places allocated to higher education institutions, the share of expenditure in GDP has fallen from 1.4% to 1%. The low growth in higher education operating support has not enabled higher education institutions to raise the pay of teaching staff in the same way as the overall wage growth in the country. Mostly, fresh Master's degree graduates receive significantly higher salaries than lecturers in the same field, including doctorate lecturers. For example, in the ICT field, according to data from last year, the pay gap is nearly 700 euros to the detriment of teachers. This year, higher education institutions received an additional EUR 5 million operating grant to ensure that the pay gap between university teachers and teachers in general education schools does not increase. But, of course, that's not enough. In the last five years, the growth in operating support for higher education has been in the order of 2 to 5% per year. This has been a marginal increase compared to both the Estonian economy and wage growth. In concluding new administrative contracts, universities have made it a condition to increase the operating grant for higher education by 15% each year, i.e. an increase in operating aid of some EUR 25 million next year. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! In a situation where the state must seek solutions to train teachers, engineers and nurses adequately, how to ensure the widest possible diversity of higher education in Estonian and academic post-growth, the question of the sustainability of the financing model of our higher education system is justified. How many free higher education are we able to pay today? How many times can a person decide on his choice of specialty free of charge? Consideration should also be given to including private money in higher education, but it is certainly important to maintain the possibility of free learning at the full time of the first higher education. Funding for higher education must become a priority theme across political parties. Good listeners! In summary, the main activities of the Ministry of Education and Research in the near future are primarily aimed at ensuring the exit of the global corona virus from the pandemic, ensuring the follow-up of teachers and improving the quality of the management of educational institutions, implementing the Estonian-language education action plan and financing higher education. These are, in my opinion, the most important steps so that we can talk about the intelligent, active, creative and entrepreneurial Estonian person. Thank you.

And it is not surprising that understanding this paradigm shift is so difficult. We are moving from a training school to an education that supports everyone's development. This concerns both the learner and the teacher. We are moving towards a self-leading learner who is aware of his own strengths and weaknesses, who can set his own goals and not under fear, but who knows why he learns what he learns and how he learns. In this paradigm shift, the teacher needs to create a suitable environment for learning. He's the one who ties back and forth, not just puts the grade, doesn't work red, or doesn't say what threshold he reached. The teacher motivates learning and supports and links back and forth learning. The prerequisite for this is indeed a competent and motivated teacher who knows what learning means, how it happens, understands human psychology. He must be a learning professional, enthusiastic about his subject, but he must be able to understand and support the student's ability to understand his strengths and develop them and choose either vocational school or upper secondary school on a completely equal footing after primary school, because he has found his strengths that he can develop, knows what his weaknesses are and is able to cope with them. The student is a partner in this new paradigm change, not an object that is being processed. He is not a vessel in whom knowledge is poured out, and for the sake of a teacher, he appreciates, for example, how much is left between his ears, but he really learns for life because he knows he needs it. Clearly, the way in which it is conducted is problematic and difficult. And the way we choose it is the subject of negotiations. But maybe it's more important to understand why we need this paradigm shift. If Estonia is at the top of the world according to the PISA educational tests, why do we want to change anything at all? I think it's behind all these misunderstandings and critical stories. The reason for this is that PISA's research shows that our children are at a good level, also shows that homes have supported children and have many books, and this is the education faith of the people. But we have problems with the school's liking, problems with mental health. We have problems with children not knowing what they like to do and what specialty or profession they could choose. We still have almost a third of children who enter working life after their education, without learning anything specially. We have a big problem with boys in education, it's a dropout, and it's that boys don't really want to learn, they're going to work. This, in turn, poses a new problem that our very good girls, who reach higher education and degrees, do not have a life partner with an appropriate level of education. It can affect ivet, it can affect migration, it can also affect the popularity of populists. And I think that all of this also shows that our education does not place much emphasis on critical thinking and does not really help us to overcome, for example, the situation in which we are in the Corona, so that people understand what scientific worldview means and know how to act on it. How do we achieve all this? The key to this is, of course, higher education and science, the development of which we need to put more emphasis than today. Thank you very much and a lot of strength for both the Minister and the Government and for all of us to make these paradigmatic changes in education. Thank you.

After all, we want a real debate, not a seeming presentation, and it is necessary to correct the format chosen at the outset and to give the ambassadors the opportunity not only to ask one but two questions and to allow speeches not only to the five ambassadors, but to all those who wish to. And the third such formal nuance, the remark that our discussion is hidden from topical issues, but the strategy could be much wider than the content of the questions asked. However, in order to be wider, it is necessary to give Parliament the opportunity to prepare. So these are technical remarks, now to the content. Of course, political problems have three phases. The first phase is when the problem, the crisis comes out, and certain decisions can solve it. The second phase is that the problem, the crisis has deepened and needs strong financial resources. The third phase is when financial resources are no longer sufficient, because the problem is so deep that even money cannot find people to help alleviate the crisis. Unfortunately, we have to admit that, in many areas of education, we are either in the second phase or between the second and third phases. Then perhaps the last chance is to try to get out of the crisis, there is no chance to miss it. There are generally at least five of these problems. The problem of teacher follow-up, which was discussed here, and which is actually a very fundamental issue. Parliament has been talking about this for several years in a row. Indeed, there is a list of the solutions that the Committee on Culture has offered to the government, and here we very much expect the Ministry of Education and Research to implement the vision that has come from Parliament. There has also been such a goodwill in favour of the Minister that we very much hope that Parliament will support it. Another major problem is the financing of higher education, including the concerns of universities of applied sciences. Here, too, I see that relatively rare situations in Estonian politics arise where universities are so rigid in their position and want to postpone the signing of contracts, and they have not just the right to do so, but also the reason. Indeed, the country did not fulfil its obligations, and at this point we are standing in this situation precisely because we did not fulfil our obligations, the country did not fulfil its obligations. The third major concern is the concerns of inclusive education. The National Audit Office has long announced that thousands of children who need additional support will not receive it within the current system. In order to solve this, we really need to take back the system that we have launched several years ago and see where it can be improved. The fourth is education in Estonian, and this is, of course, a long-standing concern. And another problem that so little is said here is the vocational education system. Today we did not hear this at all and, in fact, during last year, we talked relatively little about this topic, although for several years again the Committee on Culture has been talking about the issue of vocational education and, in the same way, has communicated its own list of possible solutions to the government. I would also like to move forward on this issue, and if we do not deal with it now, in a few years' time, the crisis will get deeper and deeper. So that's a list of burning topics. There are, of course, more of them, including, for example, the problems from the corona crisis, but on all the points that I listed, we are either in a very deep crisis or we are falling into a crisis. And there is really no time here, there is a need for action, and we would expect action from the Minister in particular, but, I stress, concerted action with Parliament, a clear vision, not just a description of the problems. ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U345	Thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to start by saying that, just as this law deals with the subject of media literacy and source criticism, then, ladies and gentlemen, we too must distinguish between things that are important in this law and things that are wanted to be made important by force, even though they are not, or have been explained, for example, in the Estonian Constitution. The main content and focus of the amendments to these laws is much more than the places that were amplified in this Chamber. The scope of the directive is extremely high. Firstly, we must realise that we have done a disservice to the Estonian media sector and its development if we have failed to update this legislation as the only Member State. Yes, there have been countries here today that are stuck with it. We are indeed among the latter, and we do not want to have tragic consequences. We would not want the area to remain within the framework of old cumbersome rules. We do not want us to be able to take into account the rapid development of digital content services; we do not want to prevent this. We would not want us to be isolated by ourselves, because if we did not change the laws, we would have different rules than [other] in the European Union. We would not want us to become an obstacle to the cross-border coverage of audiovisual media services, as well as of new digital platforms, in other parts of Europe, and so on. While all Member States consider it necessary to update their legislation and to continue to align it with the rules in force throughout the European Union, there have also been relevant changes in content according to national culture. Secondly, it is important to remember that the improvements listed in this directive were agreed in the European Union to renew the Audiovisual Media Services Directive, especially during the Estonian Presidency in 2017. So when we think about where we are today and how long it has been, it is high time we moved forward quickly with this directive, and I am glad that we have been able to introduce here amendments that are unique to our country. Our media sector is looking forward to this amendment of the law, which is a fact to operate on an equal footing. These new platforms have so far been unregulated and provide many new opportunities for successful action, as well as for the development of the Estonian media sector, including greater flexibility in advertising restrictions, protection of minors, better access to services for people with disabilities. ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U380	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I come before you with a government-led bill, an equal treatment law, and churches and parishes... [[The manager's ringing the bell.]]
ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U381	Please, let's keep the hall down!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U382	... with a draft amendment to the law. I begin by saying that non-discrimination is a principle that is enshrined in subsections 11 and 12 of our Constitution. The Equal Treatment Act is one of several laws that specify the content and nature of discrimination as well as the protection of people. I would point out that acts against discrimination are to a large extent linked to the paragraphs of the Penal Code and also to the law of the Chancellor of Justice. The Commissioner has limited powers to engage in anti-discrimination activities. Thus, the Equal Treatment Act states that the Commissioner's competence is limited to the protection of people on the grounds of nationality, race, colour, religion, belief, age, disability or sexual orientation, but specifies this as follows. In particular, discrimination against persons on grounds of nationality, race or colour is prohibited in almost all areas of life: work, vocational training, social work, health care, social security, education and also publicly available goods and services. At the same time, for groups that include people due to age, disability or sexual orientation, this is only in protected areas, namely working life and vocational training. In this respect, we have proposed in the draft that we offer uniform protection to all groups in all areas of life, resulting in groups receiving protection on the basis of nationality, race, colour, religion, belief, age, disability or sexual orientation in the following areas: work, vocational training, social work, health care, social security, education, and publicly available services and goods. What will change as a result? As a result, these groups can turn to the Commissioner for Equal Treatment in these areas of life for consultation on their rights. I would remind you that there are often vulnerable groups, such as people with special needs, people with disabilities, as well as older people, who may not have the capacity, skill or financial opportunity to go directly to court in the Constitution. The assistance and support of the Equal Treatment Commissioner is therefore essential for these people and they will be able to assess whether or not this was a discriminatory situation. It is true that we have now excluded one group, that is discrimination on the basis of social status. The reason for this is simple: we did not know how to define social status here and now unambiguously. We consulted the people of the Chancellery of the Chancellor of Justice, as we did with the Ministry of Justice, but we maintained that we are in the process of amending the Equal Treatment Act and the Gender Equality Act further this year and bringing in amendments to the next draft of social status. There is another amendment to this law concerning the compromise on churches and congregations. In particular, through the Ministry of the Interior, we discussed whether the churches and congregations are obliged to rent out the buildings and premises in their possession on the same grounds of discrimination or whether they have the right to refuse. As a compromise, we have come to the conclusion that indeed churches and congregations have the opportunity to refuse to rent premises and buildings where religious services are carried out. With churches and congregations, we actually held discussions as early as 2018, when the initial version of this draft was completed. Regrettably, this bill has been suspended and put on hold for several years. Here and now we have also taken into account the positions of churches and congregations at the time. They are available to all in the sixth section of the coordination table. In addition, I would also like to announce that there is no great burden on the state budget coming up with this draft. The instruments necessary for the implementation of the draft relate in particular to communication activities and staff costs in the Commissioner's Office for Equal Treatment. These amounts amount to EUR 100280 per year. As has been said, they come from the Social Ministry's own resources. This is where I'm gonna finish and give you a chance to ask questions. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U482	Thank you. Ladies and gentlemen, This draft was discussed by the Committee on Constitutional Affairs at its meeting on Tuesday 25 January. This bill was presented by Signe Riisalo in an analogous way to today's [exhibition], so I will not repeat it. Some of the questions have already been heard in this Chamber, but now there is an overview. Toomas Kivimägi asked whether, in essence, this bill would bring the Equality Act into line with § 12 of the Constitution. Signe Riisalo confirmed that yes, it could be said that it would bring better alignment. Although discrimination is generally prohibited, as has been mentioned, access to the assistance and support of the Commissioner has so far been limited and the draft opens up the supporting role of the Commissioner to all groups in all areas of life listed in the law. He acknowledged that there was indeed a lack of social status, because, in cooperation with the Office of the Ministry of Justice and the Chancellor of Justice, there was no way to define this social status unambiguously. Kalle Grünthal also raised in the Commission the problem of the possibility of obtaining loans for older people. Signe Riisalo said it was a big problem. In addition, there is a similar, analogous problem in the area of life insurance. In fact, this bill creates an opportunity for a person to turn to the Equal Treatment Commissioner, who can assess whether this is a proportionate restriction, whether the restriction is justified and on a fair basis. It'll give you an extra chance. Here I would like to emphasise precisely what came up in the Commission several times again, whether Article 12 of the Constitution and the Equal Treatment Act say that no distinctions must be made. No, it's not a draft or a law, it's not allowed to make any distinctions, but they must be justified and fair, as § 11 of the Constitution says. This is where the Commissioner for Equality comes in. The issue of the rental market was also widely discussed. I pointed out that it was difficult for families with children to get [rental apartments]. On the other hand, Mr Paul Puustusmaa found that the owner was not obliged to rent his own home to anyone and could base his own decision on anything, the principle of trade in people's own property and business freedom is that if you come to rent his own house or apartment, he himself knows whether or not to let this person have his own property. In addition, in the rental question Urmas Reinsalu asked whether, now that a person wants to rent an apartment, for example, neo-Nazis and Satanists, a person does not have the right to refuse to rent his or her own apartment because it is discrimination. According to this logic, the country that rents out its rooms, said Urmas Reinsalu, should start to allow meeting rooms, for example, for anti-state activities for Russian extremists, and this is how it gets out of hand. In addition, Urmas Reinsalu studied that if restrictions are not imposed on the basis of sexual orientation, does this mean affection in any choice. For example, does the initiator suggest that a family with children should not refuse to rent a dwelling or a room in an apartment to paedophile? Toomas Kivimägi found the pedophilia on the surface of an example that there really should be a limit somewhere. If a pedophile wants to rent the dwelling before the rental agreement is concluded, then the person is forced to enter into the contract, for example, this may lead to the resentment of the neighbors. Couldn't these situations lead to social condemnation and resentment in the opposite direction, doesn't it create social tension? In response to these comments, Signe Riisalo found that there are really problems in the rental market, and there are other [such] groups besides families with children, and indeed, if there is a way to introduce social status into the law, that would be very reasonable. In addition, Signe Riisalo in the Commission considered that the grounds for refusal should be justified and proportionate. If, for example, the lessor suffers reputational damage, social pressure or social disapproval, he or she can refuse to enter into the contract. The refusal shall be proportionate in such cases. He found that, since the paedophile issue was raised, that once the paedophile has realized its affection for the minor, it is criminal, punishable, and there is legislation that later allows [his freedom of action] to be restricted. But if he has not harmed anyone and restrains that affection, then he is simply an ordinary person who cannot be labeled and condemned forever. Toomas Kivimägi considered that the integration of a paedophile into society must not be at the expense of the reputation of other people. I added in the area of rental that § 12 of the Constitution applies, and applies independently of legislation, which means that, in fact, today, when a room is rented out in public and a peaceful meeting is held there, such as neo-Nazis, Satanists, EKRE, Reform Party, this cannot be refused by the lessor. We can imagine that if we allow this to be done on an arbitrary basis, when the elections are coming, the local government may decide that it rents to a political party publicly [outlet] space and does not rent to another party. That is not what we would like to get to, and that is why we do not allow discrimination. I also mentioned that reputational damage is sometimes problematic. For example, when we talk about former prisoners, everyone is afraid of reputational damage, no one wants to rent them an apartment, and these people stay on the street forever. That's a fair country you shouldn't want. Let us recall that the right to a home is also present in the Declaration of Human Rights, which is exactly the same as our constitutional value. In fact, with this draft, we are not discussing whether Article 12 of the Constitution will change in any way at all, but how and which groups will receive help from the Commissioner for Equality. In addition to the issue of rent, the problems of the temples were raised from two angles. First of all, once again I mentioned that yes indeed, the church is a specific organization, forcing the church to give its sanctuary, for example, to a conference of atheists or to an act of blasphemy, is probably inhumane. But here, too, the question arises: if we make an exception for one ideological community, why do we treat others differently? Could trade unions, for example, also have such protection that they would not allow an anti-trade union conference in their rented premises? In addition, the question arose as to whether the school was specific, whether it should be mentioned separately, whether the school had to rent its own premises for a strip show, for example. Why are we making an exception for the church, but the school doesn't have an exception as to how this is justified? Indeed, Signe Riisalo found that yes, the issue of exceptions is not complicated. His personal preference would be that there should be no exceptions, but it was discussed, this exception was reached as a result of compromises and, in fact, it was a little designed, so far there have been no such problems. However, Signe Riisalo mentioned that the church was concerned that this problem might suddenly arise in the future, even though it did not exist. In the case of school, the Commission concluded that, as long as school events take place, children must be protected, that there are specific rules in place, but that if the school rents its own premises outside the study period, it is public space. But, of course, the school's rules of procedure allow certain restrictions to be imposed. It remained a little open in the Commission. I'd like to step aside for a moment, comment, I've checked it in the meantime. In fact, the school can also do certain restrictions on the use of its premises outside the study. This is justified by the student's right to use school premises outside the study. The student is always entitled to use the school premises outside the study, because perhaps he or she does not have the opportunity to do homework, somewhere this possibility must be available, the school rooms must guarantee it, and therefore the school must ensure that there is a suitable atmosphere for children at all times. Since it already exists, a separate distinction does not necessarily have to be made here. In addition, it was mentioned about the church that Urmas Reinsalu had a conversation with representatives of the Estonian Council of Churches during the meeting of the Commission, who stated that the Ministry of Social Affairs had never approached them as partners. He certainly considered this to be discrimination and suggested that the Committee on Constitutional Affairs invite representatives of the Estonian Council of Churches to the second-reading debate. Signe Riisalo replied that the Ministry of Social Affairs is preparing and coordinating its draft with all relevant partners, and the Estonian Council of Churches has coordinated this draft with its comments. All these comments can be found in the material submitted to the Committee on Constitutional Affairs as an annex to the explanatory memorandum. All these comments are further explained by the Ministry of Social Affairs. Okay. In addition, the question arose as to the objective of non-discrimination, which was asked by both Urmas Reinsalu and Paul Puustusmaa, who found that the concept of discrimination itself is very confusing and the letter itself, this concept is so scattered that, in essence, all social relations can be subject to discrimination. He felt that the exception of churches and congregations was not really necessary; instead, it could be abolished in such a way that all individuals and associations would be entitled to refuse to use their property if the event or activity involved did not correspond to the moral sense of the owner. Nele Wheat, a legal adviser to the Ministry of Social Affairs, replied to such a broader questionnaire on non-discrimination than such, which gave an example. Let's say the store owns some kind of property that the store sells, the owner does what he wants, and he wants to sell it. The store is hired by an employee who makes a deal with the client, selling him some kind of goods. If the seller were to sell the property according to his convictions, for example, he did not want to sell the goods to black people, that would be discrimination. The idea of non-discrimination is that minorities do not lose their services so that minorities can live, they can consume everything that is offered in public spaces. If you start from what your beliefs are, it's allowed until your beliefs begin to limit someone else's freedoms and rights so that someone loses important public services. This is what the democratic rule of law must defend. Urmas Reinsalu also asked whether there was an age limit and whether there was an age limit for receiving free higher education. If the draft extends the prohibition of discrimination in education in relation to age, will these age limits conflict with this draft? Kaisa Knight, Head of Equal Treatment Policy of the Ministry of Social Affairs, explained that this is part of the Ministry of Education and Research, but access to education is a question of proportionate consideration, and if the legislator has imposed certain restrictions there, this is based on the clash of two laws and the weighting of proportionality. In addition, Urmas Reinsalu asked that, when clergymen perform civil status duties as a public service, do they have the right to refuse to perform civil status activities as a public service on the basis of sexual orientation, such as refusing to perform certain weddings? Signe Riisalo replied that § 6 of the Family Act states that a legitimate clergyman has the right to refuse to enter into a marriage if the married person does not meet the conditions for entering into a marriage after a religion in the church, congregation, or association of congregations. Then Urmas Reinsalu asked what it was like to order a clergyman, whether there could be discriminatory provisions, for example, in the recruitment of a clergyman. Nele Wheat explained that there is already a provision in force to that effect, according to which different treatment of a person is not regarded as discrimination where a person's religion or belief is essential because of the nature of the activity or the conditions attached to it. So here again is the question that restrictions may be imposed, but these restrictions must be justified. Then decisions: propose a draft to be put on the plenary agenda on 9 February 2022, adopted by consensus, and propose the completion of the first reading by 5 votes to 3, with 0 abstentions. Toomas Kivimägi proposed to appoint Oudekki Loone as the representative of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, Paul Puustusmaa proposed to appoint Kalle Grünthal as the representative of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, and Urmas Reinsalu proposed to appoint Lauri Läänemets as the representative of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. ParlaMint-EE_2022-02-09_U494	Mr President of the sitting of the Riigikogu. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I am pleased to stand before the honourable Riigikogu to introduce you to the very long-awaited draft, the so-called draft harmonisation of special services 508 in the field of internal security. Above all, the draft deals with different social guarantees and service regulations for internal security staff and volunteers. The draft amends 14 laws. The preparation of the draft plan started already in 2017 and has been long awaited in the field. The following is an overview of the major changes that the draft will bring. We shall equalise the benefits of volunteers and their family members with those of professional servants and their family members. Volunteers stand shoulder to shoulder with police and rescuers and also risk their lives and health. Equal responsibility must also guarantee equal benefits and treatment. Currently, voluntary benefits are lower because there is no distinction between the loss of life or the loss of working capacity caused by an attack or accident. At present, in both cases, if voluntary deaths occur, their loved ones will be paid ten years' wages. For example, a member of the professional's family is paid 20 years' salary in the event of an attack. Volunteers also have lower compensation for reduced capacity to work. A special servant shall be entitled to a benefit of up to 13 years' salary. The maximum voluntary benefit shall be the average salary of up to seven years. The assistant police officer is not compensated on the basis of his actual salary, but on the basis of the police officer's lowest salary. In the future, the allowance for an auxiliary police officer will also be based on his actual salary and the maximum amount of the allowance will be three times the average salary. We're creating a separate crisis role for relief officers. The crisis reserve to be made up of relief police officers is needed to ensure that in crisis situations, the PBGB and the Ministry of the Interior have sufficient resources to manage the escalation of the crisis or the negative effects of the crisis. The Assistant Police Officer with the role of crisis shall have the right to refuse to work or exercise public authority with the permanent employer during the resolution period and to receive compensation for the loss of remuneration or salary. Support police officers assigned to the crisis reserve are not included in the resources of the Defence League or the Defence Forces, for example. This precludes so-called double planning. The aim is to include 640 people in the crisis reserve in four years. After 2023, we also allow the rescuers to receive an old-age pension benefit for health reasons and extend it to five years instead of the previous three years. Rescue workers must feel that the state understands the nature of their work and appreciates their contribution. As a result of their work to harm their health, we need to support rescue workers who are elderly before their retirement pension, if their health is no longer allowed to continue with their professional work. This means that if, for medical reasons, it is no longer possible for the savior to continue his work, the state will pay an old-age pension waiting allowance up to five years before the retirement age. We are in a situation where the decision adopted in 2008 would put an end to this social guarantee. In summary, we are talking about four to five additional rescuers per year who need support for health reasons. After 2023, we also allow the rescuers to collect an increase in their old-age pension. This is a 10-50% pension supplement for rescue workers with a minimum period of ten years of employment. This means that, for example, for 25 years of service, old-age pension increases by 25%. The change in the pension increase will allow retired rescue workers to take better care of their health, which may be worse due to work detrimental to health than those retired from other professions. We also see that the wages of the rescuers are not yet high enough to allow us to abandon this system. Therefore, we consider it necessary to extend the study to 2043. After 2025, we also allow rescuers to receive a pension for the years they have earned in parallel, i.e. the so-called VAP, while continuing their professional work. Otherwise, there would be a situation in which the savior would have to choose whether to continue in a new profession and receive a VAP or continue saving without a VAP. As a country, we cannot afford to lose men who are fully viable and extremely professionally necessary to another job. The amendment ensures that rescuers do not leave the service. Nor does the change entail a financial cost, since the State will in any case have to pay out the VAP to the rescuer who has left the service. There may be a cost if no change is made because it is difficult to find new rescuers and their training is expensive. The need for all three previous amendments has been stressed by the Rescue Board and the Estonian Salvation Area Workers' Union. We will continue to collect retirement pensions for the years we have earned until 2036. This means that up to 2036 rescuers, policemen and prison officials will collect retirement pensions for the years they have earned. Background: Getting a VAP requires 25 years of service, no other requirements such as age or reduced capacity for work. Similarly, special police pensions were abolished in 2020. Why is this change necessary? At present, the VAP provides a justification for not having to be high enough for the person entering the savior's profession, because in 25 years' time, the VAP will become available. We see the problem that young people no longer want to come to work knowing that in 25 years, they will receive a certain benefit in addition to their salary. The special service's salary must be dignified and valued since the first year. That is why the savior's salary was raised by nearly 12% in 2022 and we need to move forward with this increase at least in the same step to reach the target set for 2025. A valuable salary from the first year on will ultimately guarantee both a higher total income and a pension for the future. The amendment has been made in agreement with the Rescue Board, the PBGB and the Estonian Rescue Area Workers' Trade Union. I would point out that there has indeed been very good cooperation with the various trade unions in making these amendments. Let's put the policemen's salary components into the basic salary. This means that the pay system for police officers will be placed on the same footing with the general public service wage system. At present, the basic salary of a police officer consists of a grade salary, a grade [pay], a regional fee and a level playing field. From now on, the basic salary of a police officer does not consist of components, but is established as a single number. The policeman is paid basic salary, which includes all the current components of the salary. The amendment also means that the police pension will in future be based on the total salary of a police officer, including a step salary, a grade fee, a regional fee and a compensatory fee. The amendments ensure equal treatment of police officers and other special servants, such as prosecutors, defence forces, when determining pensions. Just in case, I mention that the special pension system for police officers has been abolished since 2020. Those who worked before will receive it at the end of their service. We will make the posts of deputies for the Directors-General of the Offices fixed-term. This means that the five-year term of employment will be fixed at the posts of the Deputy Directors-General of the PBGB, the Rescue Board, the Alert Centre and the Defence Police Department. Currently, the service of the Director-General and Prefects of the PBA is of a fixed-term nature, but, for example, the posts of the Deputy Directors-General are of limited duration. The amendment will help the institution to better organise its activities when, for example, a new Director-General comes into service, it will also encourage rotation and increased competence and commitment, it will support the motivation of top management and prevent fatigue and health problems caused by stressful and stressful work. We bring the redundancy allowances for special servants on the same basis as for other officials and staff. Under the current regime, policemen, defence forces and prison officers are paid redundancy benefits, depending on their seniority, for a period of two to ten months, for other officials and for staff, for a month's salary. This distinction is unfounded today because, like all other redundant officials and staff, special servants are entitled to unemployment benefit. We create an opportunity to check the background of a candidate or person involved in the provision of a service to the Ministry of the Interior or to the institutions of his or her area of government. It is necessary to check the data in order to assess the suitability of a person to take up work, internship or service in the administration. The amendment will ensure that there are reliable and sound people working in the administration, who can also be entrusted with information relevant to national security and security. The Police and Border Guard Board will be in charge of the inspection. I think it is important to stress that, compared to the legislation in force, the data collections are not extended, but extended to those whose data are checked. We will introduce a distinction between the release of a police officer suspected or accused in criminal proceedings. The amendment follows from the Supreme Court's judgment of 12 April 2021, according to which the court declared the provision that a police officer suspected or accused in criminal proceedings must be released from service. The amendment allows the driver to decide whether to transfer a police officer to another post or to withdraw him from service. We allow police officers to be transferred to aggregate working hours. This is what we do in order to ensure national security, defence, where necessary, natural disaster, fire, disaster or disaster prevention or remedy its consequences, and to prevent mass disorder. Such cases, if a police officer needs to be transferred to aggregated working hours, are rare in practice and hopefully will continue to be so. This is a very brief overview of the major changes to the draft. I consider it necessary, once again, to thank all the parties involved, the social partners, the internal security authorities, the people of the ministry, the previous ministers who have all contributed to this package. I have already touched on trade unions with whom there has been very good cooperation. Thanks for listening, and I'm ready to answer the questions.
